The absence of psychotic symptoms and hospitalization does not rule out either Bipolar I or II Disorder, as both can present without these features. I ended up making reckless business investments without properly reviewing them and went on unrestrained shopping sprees that caused severe financial distress for me and my family.\n\nDoctor: During those four months of high energy and impulsivity, did you ever feel that your mood shifted to an extremely elevated or irritable state, where you might have appeared abnormally happy or angry to others, to the point of causing marked impairment in social or occupational functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mood shifted unpredictably during those four months. I would feel abnormally happy, euphoric, and full of energy, but then suddenly become extremely irritable and angry over the smallest things. People around me definitely noticed these extreme shifts, and it caused a lot of tension.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many days out of those four months you spent in this extremely elevated or irritable state, and did you require any intervention or support from others to manage your behavior during that time?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'm not sure of the exact number of days, but it felt like it was ongoing for almost the entire four months. I didn't require any professional intervention or hospitalization, though my family definitely had to step in to try and manage the financial mess I was making.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are an assistant analyst connecting the [Symptom Profile] with the doctor's question.\nDetermine which part of the profile (chief complaint, associated symptoms, social history, etc.)\nthe doctor's **last question** is related to.\n\n[Symptom Profile]\n▸ hypomanic_episode\n  - Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli: Attention is immediately drawn to trivial and unimportant stimuli nearby (e.g., noise, movement).\n  - Frequent Task Switching: Unable to finish one task and constantly shifting attention to new things, starting multiple tasks simultaneously.\n  - Inability to Sustain Attention: Unable to focus on any activity for long, with attention constantly being scattered.\n  - Unrestrained Buying Sprees: Spending excessive amounts of money or shopping recklessly to a degree that creates financial distress for self and family.\n  - Foolish Business Investments: Making rash business investments or ventures without sufficient review.\n  - Sexual Indiscretions: Engaging in indiscriminate or risky sexual behavior that is out of character.\n  - Impulsive Risky Behavior: Excessively dangerous or foolish behavior, such as reckless driving or drug use, without considering the consequences.\n  Duration: 4 months\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Purposeless repetitive behaviors such as inability to sit still, pacing, hand-wringing, or picking at skin.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Motor): Visible slowing of general body movements, such as walking speed or picking up objects.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Speech): Long response latency or slowed speech when asked a question.\n  - Feelings of Worthlessness: The feeling that one is useless and has no value.\n  - Ruminative Guilt: A thought pattern of obsessively replaying past minor mistakes or flaws and constantly reproaching oneself.\n  - Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt: Feeling excessive responsibility for trivial matters or daily events and believing one deserves punishment.\n  - Delusional Guilt: Guilt reaching a delusional level, disconnected from reality, involving feeling responsible for unrelated unfortunate events.\n  - Recurrent Thoughts of Death: Passive wish to die (e.g., 'I wish I wouldn't wake up') without active intent to kill oneself.\n  - Suicidal Ideation: Recurring thoughts of 'I want to die' or 'I want to kill myself' without a specific plan.\n  - However, the patient's report of having 'some beliefs during my depression that felt disconnected from reality' and an 'intense, completely unrealistic sense of guilt' suggests the possibility of psychotic features, which is why Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features and Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features are now also considered. The absence of hallucinations and delusions during the manic episodes does not rule out the possibility of psychotic features during depressive episodes, hence the inclusion of these diagnoses in the candidate list.", "is_final": false}

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are an assistant analyst connecting the [Symptom Profile] with the doctor's question.\nDetermine which part of the profile (chief complaint, associated symptoms, social history, etc.)\nthe doctor's **last question** is related to.\n\n[Symptom Profile]\n▸ hypomanic_episode\n  - Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli: Attention is immediately drawn to trivial and unimportant stimuli nearby (e.g., noise, movement).\n  - Frequent Task Switching: Unable to finish one task and constantly shifting attention to new things, starting multiple tasks simultaneously.\n  - Inability to Sustain Attention: Unable to focus on any activity for long, with attention constantly being scattered.\n  - Unrestrained Buying Sprees: Spending excessive amounts of money or shopping recklessly to a degree that creates financial distress for self and family.\n  - Foolish Business Investments: Making rash business investments or ventures without sufficient review.\n  - Sexual Indiscretions: Engaging in indiscriminate or risky sexual behavior that is out of character.\n  - Impulsive Risky Behavior: Excessively dangerous or foolish behavior, such as reckless driving or drug use, without considering the consequences.\n  Duration: 4 months\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  -
